This dictated the whole design: `.ride-map-container` (containing `<MapView>`) and the Profile pane's own wrapper (containing `GradientColoursDisclosure`) are each a **single, always-rendered** element — present with the same shape in both idle and active, and both `.ride-content-area`-child slots the same on every render regardless of `activeView` — with idle/active and Map/Profile differences expressed entirely via conditional `className`/inline `style`/`aria-hidden` on that same stable element, never via branching that wraps either of them differently. `RidingScreen.tsx`'s idle branch is otherwise byte-for-byte unchanged from before this slice; only its map-preview div and `.ride-profile-panel` div now sit as the two children of the same unconditionally-present `.ride-content-area` wrapper (base, non-`--immersive` variant during idle — a plain flex column reproducing the exact 16px gap those two elements already had as ordinary flex siblings of `.screen`, a pure relocation with no idle-state visual change).
    - **State.** One new local `useState<"map" | "profile">("map")` in `RidingScreen.tsx` (`activeView`), reset to `"map"` only inside the **existing** idle-only branch of `handleStart` (the same branch that already resets `explicitFeatureSelection`/`selectedGradientSegment`) — defaults to Map on a genuinely fresh Start and on an explicit Resume-riding tap, but deliberately not on the mid-ride "Try again" retry path (the same handler's `else` branch, untouched) — a rider mid-GPS-error on Profile is not yanked back to Map. A Pause→relaunch→Resume cycle gets a fresh `"map"` default for free, since this whole screen unmounts on Pause. Not persisted to storage, per the item's own scope (presentation-only local state).
    - **Both panes (Map, Profile) stay permanently mounted; toggling is CSS-only (inline `visibility`/`pointerEvents`, plus `aria-hidden`), never conditional unmount.** Each pane is `position: absolute; inset: 0` inside a shared `position: relative` content area (`.ride-content-area--immersive`), so both panes always occupy the identical, constant-size box regardless of which is active — the map's rendered pixel size never changes on toggle, so there is no `<MapView>` resize risk to reason about at all (not even relying on `ResizeObserver` spec behaviour for a 0→nonzero transition, since the box is never zero). The toggle uses **inline `style`, not a CSS class**, for the visibility/pointer-events pair: `vite.config.ts`'s test config sets `test: { css: false }`, so component tests never load `index.css` — a class-driven visual state (as this file already documents happening once, for `GradientColourSwatch`) would be invisible to jsdom assertions, whereas inline style is always readable via `getComputedStyle`/`toHaveStyle`, and `@testing-library`'s `getByRole` correctly excludes `visibility: hidden` subtrees from queries, giving real, testable proof that inactive-pane controls become unreachable. `aria-hidden` is added defensively alongside it.
    - **A genuine DOM-duplication bug found and fixed during test-writing, not merely theorised.** The first design gated the compact manoeuvre cue (see below) purely on urgency (`!idle && nextManoeuvre !== null && urgency !== "normal"`), reasoning that mounting it unconditionally inside the always-present, merely-`aria-hidden`-toggled Profile pane was harmless. A real component test proved otherwise: `screen.getByText(...)` (unlike `getByRole`) does **not** respect `aria-hidden`/`visibility`, so whenever the SAME manoeuvre instruction text happened to be near/imminent while Map was selected, it existed **twice** in the DOM simultaneously — once in the full Map-view `RidingNextManoeuvrePanel`, once in the always-mounted-but-hidden Profile-pane compact cue — breaking `getByText`-based assertions with "found multiple elements." Fixed by additionally gating the compact cue's render on `activeView === "profile"` (unlike the panes themselves, the cue is stateless with no `GradientColoursDisclosure`-style continuity requirement, so conditionally mounting/unmounting it is completely safe and costs nothing) — eliminating the duplication outright rather than just working around it in tests.
    - **Manoeuvre panel is Map-exclusive; status strip and completion panel are shared; Profile gets a compact manoeuvre cue only at near/imminent urgency (confirmed with the user before implementation).** The full `RidingNextManoeuvrePanel` has no idle counterpart and holds no internal state, so it renders only when `!idle && activeView === "map"` — matching the Profile mock-ups, which never show it, and giving Profile the mock-ups' full elevation-focused layout whenever the next manoeuvre is merely "normal" urgency. When it isn't — i.e. `classifyManoeuvreUrgency` (an existing exported pure function from `navigation/nextManoeuvre.ts`, unchanged, moved nowhere) classifies the _same_ already-computed `nextManoeuvre.remainingDistanceMetres` RidingScreen already passes to the full panel as `"near"` or `"imminent"` — a small new `RidingCompactManoeuvreCue` component (`src/ui/riding/RidingCompactManoeuvreCue.tsx`, new) renders at the top of the Profile pane's `.ride-elevation-section`: a direction icon (reusing the existing `ManoeuvreIcon`), a CSS-ellipsis-truncated ("shortened") one-line instruction, and the distance. `genericManoeuvreLabel` (the per-type fallback instruction text) was extracted out of `RidingNextManoeuvrePanel.tsx` into a new shared `src/ui/riding/manoeuvreLabels.ts` — not merely exported from the panel file — because exporting a non-component function alongside a component trips `react-refresh/only-export-components`, a warning this slice chose to eliminate rather than tolerate; `formatManoeuvreDistance` (already exported from `routeSummary.ts`) is imported directly, no new formatting logic. `RidingCompactManoeuvreCue` never switches the view to Map and never forces document scroll (it sits inside the Profile pane's own bounded content, ahead of `elevationSectionBody`). Because `showCompactManoeuvreCue` is a plain boolean recomputed every render from the live `nextManoeuvre`/urgency (not a one-shot decision), it disappears automatically the instant the manoeuvre's urgency returns to `"normal"` or the selection advances past it — no explicit "hide" logic is needed. `RidingStatusStrip` and `RidingRouteCompletionPanel` render unconditionally (as before this slice, gated only on their own existing conditions, never on `activeView`) so they're visible from either view — directly satisfying the requirement that completion must "remain readily discoverable... without an automatic view switch." Visual order when Map is selected is status → manoeuvre (full) → completion → map, matching the required priority list; when Profile is selected it's status → completion → profile content, with the compact cue appearing inside the Profile pane only during near/imminent urgency.
    - **The "Map follow paused." toast moved into the map itself as a non-layout-affecting overlay — a real regression found and fixed, not a design preference.** The toast originally stayed in its item-55 position (a shared-area `<p role="status">`, a normal-flow sibling above the map). A real e2e test (`e2e/ridingCamera.spec.ts`'s "re-pressing Follow location..." test) failed consistently (5/5 repeats) because the toast's own transient appear/dismiss cycle (it auto-dismisses after its existing ~3 s timeout) measurably **resized** `.ride-map-container--immersive`'s flex-fill box — the shared stack's height directly determines how much space the map's `flex: 1` has left — which in turn shifted the reported camera centre via the follow-offset recalculation on resize, by an amount exceeding the test's `1e-6` tolerance (confirmed directly via temporary debug instrumentation: the map's own bounding box measurably differed, e.g. height 396 vs. 329px, between "toast showing" and "toast dismissed"). Fixed by moving `{camera.showPausedToast ? <p role="status" className="ride-map-paused-toast">...} : null}` to be a child of `.ride-map-container` itself (after the zoom/camera control clusters), styled as `position: absolute; bottom: 8px; left: 50%; transform: translateX(-50%)` — a genuine overlay that never affects layout height, and is naturally Map-view-exclusive as a side effect (no reason to show it while Profile is selected, and it's automatically hidden along with the rest of the Map pane). Re-verified: 15/15 clean across repeated runs after the fix.
    - **Two further real overflow/click-interception bugs found via e2e failures, both fixed with narrowly-scoped, evidence-backed CSS — not by loosening any assertion.** First: `MapView`'s own status banners (e.g. `data-testid="map-fallback-banner"`, shown when tiles fail to load) have no explicit CSS positioning at all — plain, unstyled block `<div>`s. Under the old `--active` fixed/clamped-height sizing this was harmless (surplus space simply pushed a normal-flow banner further down an ordinarily-scrolling page); under `--immersive`'s flex-fill sizing, an overflowing banner could extend far enough below the map's own box to visually cover, and intercept clicks on, the Map/Profile switcher sitting immediately after `.ride-content-area` in the DOM (`e2e/gradientColouring.spec.ts`'s pre-ride-briefing test caught this directly: `<div data-testid="map-fallback-banner"> ... intercepts pointer events`). Fixed with `.ride-content-area--immersive > .ride-map-container--immersive { overflow: hidden; }` — deliberately different from `.ride-map-container--overview`'s own documented choice to omit `overflow: hidden` (that rule's own comment is about a `position: absolute` element, e.g. `.map-attribution`, whose edge could sit flush against the box and get clipped — confirmed not applicable here, since attribution stays within bounds by its own design, per `e2e/layout.spec.ts`'s pre-existing `isFullyWithin` proof, re-run and still passing). Second, found by the new `e2e/ridingMapProfileViews.spec.ts`'s own portrait-to-landscape test at an unusually short landscape viewport (844×390): `.ride-map-container--immersive`'s own `min-height: 160px` floor, when the genuinely available space is smaller than that, forces the browser to recompute the element's `bottom` offset away from `0` (the documented behaviour for an absolutely-positioned element with both `top`/`bottom` set whose `min-height` cannot be satisfied within that inset) — extending the map's own box past `.ride-content-area--immersive`'s own bottom edge and, since that parent had no `overflow: hidden` of its own, into the switcher's screen position, again intercepting clicks. Fixed with a second, one-level-up `overflow: hidden` on `.ride-content-area--immersive` itself, as a defensive backstop — verified to have no effect on ordinary rendering, since in normal (non-edge-case) conditions neither absolutely-positioned, `inset: 0` child ever extends past its own parent's bounds in the first place.
    - **CSS additions (`src/index.css`).** `.screen.riding-fixed-shell` (`height: 100vh`, `@supports (height: 100dvh) { height: 100dvh }`, `overflow: hidden`) — applied only to `RidingScreen`'s own active-branch `<section>`, never to `FreeRoamScreen` (which keeps its ordinary scrolling `.screen`, per this item's own route-Riding-only scope) and never to the idle/pre-ride branch. `.app-shell:has(.riding-fixed-shell) { padding-bottom: 0; }`, mirroring the existing top-padding cancellation (`.app-shell:has(.riding-immersive-header)`) for the identical inset-double-counting reason — the bottom safe-area inset is instead folded into `.ride-immersive-switcher`'s own `padding-bottom: calc(var(--safe-area-inset-bottom) + var(--space-8))`. `.ride-content-area`/`.ride-content-area--immersive` (the new wrapper described above), `.ride-map-container--immersive` (replaces `--active`'s fixed/clamped height with a genuine flex-fill — **a deliberate departure from `--active`'s own `round(nearest, 46dvh, 20px)` snap**, documented as such: that technique only applies to a static `calc()`/`dvh` expression, and a flex-computed size can't be expressed that way; verified instead with the real drag-rotate/pitch gesture e2e coverage already established by items 21/23/34, which passed unmodified against the new sizing — `--active` itself is untouched and stays exactly as today for `FreeRoamScreen` and the idle preview), `.ride-profile-pane--immersive` (`overflow-y: auto`, the bounded internal-scroll fallback for secondary Profile content under very large text or an unusually short viewport — the header, shared status stack and switcher never scroll; only this pane's own content can, and only when it genuinely doesn't fit), `.ride-immersive-switcher`/`-button`/`.is-selected` (mirrors `.riding-immersive-header`'s own edge-to-edge negative-margin technique — safe here since the switcher is a plain flex item, not `position: sticky`, so the documented sticky/negative-margin interaction on `.app-header--sticky` doesn't apply), `.ride-map-paused-toast`, `.ride-compact-manoeuvre-cue`/`-instruction`/`-distance`.

### Riding elevation enhancement

59. **Elevation and recognised-climb discrepancy investigation**
    - Field observation (unverified against ground truth, not yet a confirmed defect): on one ride, ACN's planned figure was approximately 640 m ascent versus approximately 540 m recorded by a Garmin watch and approximately 510 m reported by Google for a comparable route. Some recognised climb sections subjectively felt like they started later than the physical climb on the road.
    - Confirmed current implementation (recorded here as ground truth for any future analysis, not as an admission of a defect): ascent/descent totals (`src/navigation/elevation.ts`) resample raw route-point elevation at `RESAMPLE_STEP_METRES = 20` m (flat-extrapolating), apply a centred moving-average smoothing window of `SMOOTHING_WINDOW_SAMPLES = 5` samples (~100 m at that spacing), and accumulate with a `MIN_ASCENT_DELTA_METRES = 1` m reversal threshold — this pipeline runs independently of, though sharing constants/helpers with, the gradient/climb-detection pipeline below. Local gradient and climb/descent detection (`src/navigation/gradient.ts`, `src/navigation/routeFeatures.ts`) share the same 20 m resample step and reuse the elevation module's smoothing window; local gradient is fitted by least-squares regression over a `GRADE_BASELINE_WINDOW_METRES = 100` m centred window (minimum usable window `MIN_GRADE_WINDOW_METRES = 40` m near a run's edges), with runs split apart by gaps over `MAX_ELEVATION_GAP_METRES = 500` m and short (`MIN_SEGMENT_LENGTH_METRES = 80` m) classification flicker suppressed. Climb recognition requires length ≥ `MIN_FEATURE_LENGTH_METRES = 500` m, average gradient ≥ `MIN_CLIMB_AVERAGE_GRADIENT_PERCENT = 3` %, and Garmin's own published `climbScore ≥ MIN_CLIMB_SCORE = 1500`; a boundary only confirms once a reversal exceeds `REVERSAL_BRIDGE_ELEVATION_METRES = 10` m or persists past `REVERSAL_BRIDGE_DISTANCE_METRES = 200` m of distance since the tracked extremum (a flat lead-in/plateau within `FLAT_EPSILON_METRES = 0.01` m of the current extremum is treated as neither extending nor reversing). None of the above trusts a provider-supplied ascent/descent summary — ACN always recomputes from route-point elevation itself, per this project's long-standing "do not sum raw positive elevation noise" GPX rule.
    - Treat this strictly as an evidence-gathering investigation, not a confirmed provider-data defect, and not a request to retune any constant above yet.
    - Required investigation before any tuning proposal: compare the exact ACN-exported planned GPX for the ride in question against the corresponding Garmin-recorded GPX/FIT activity, aligned by distance and geography — ask the user to supply both files explicitly for a dedicated future analysis task; keep them local, never commit or retain them, and allow the user to trim privacy-sensitive sections first, per this project's "never commit personal GPX files" rule. Report, from that comparison: route-point spacing in each file, ACN's raw-vs-smoothed elevation profile, ACN's cumulative ascent under the current algorithm above, and exactly where recognised climb boundaries differ from where the rider judged the physical climb to start/end. Treat Garmin and Google's own figures as comparison data points, not ground truth — they may use different elevation sources (barometric vs. digital-elevation-model), their own smoothing, and their own ascent thresholds, none of which ACN can inspect. Get the user to clarify, with a concrete reproducible case, what "later" means for the climb-boundary complaint: the coloured map boundary (`routeFeatureLayer.ts`), the highlighted elevation-chart section, or the automatic active-Climb view's own entry point (`climbElevationView.ts`) — three distinct presentation layers over the same underlying boundary that could diverge from each other even if the boundary itself is correct.
    - Only after that analysis should a separate, later-numbered implementation slice propose any change to smoothing, the reversal threshold, climb-boundary hysteresis, or elevation-source handling — any such proposal must quantify its effect across several representative routes (not just the one ride that prompted this), and add deterministic regression fixtures rather than tuning purely to match one ride's Garmin/Google numbers.

### Battery and performance investigation

60. **Battery consumption investigation and possible battery-saving mode**
    - Field observation (hypothesis-level, not a confirmed cause): an approximately two-hour installed iPhone PWA route ride saw battery fall from roughly 90% to below 20%. A separate, approximately ten-minute walking free-roam test (already recorded under item 42's own manual-acceptance entry) showed no obvious heat or battery drain, but was too short to validate representative endurance.
    - Record contributing factors as hypotheses only, not conclusions: high screen brightness/keeping the display awake; the app's current light presentation, particularly on an OLED device; continuous high-accuracy `watchPosition` use; MapLibre rendering and camera-update/easing frequency; cellular tile downloads, weak signal, or other device/environmental conditions; device model, battery health, and ambient temperature. A PWA cannot directly control hardware screen brightness; browser geolocation has no dependable application-controlled update interval; and the project must not trade away navigation reliability merely to claim better battery life.
    - Stage the work, and do not conflate the stages:
      1. **Measurement and diagnosis:** define a repeatable 60–120 minute real-device test procedure recording phone model, iOS version, battery health, system appearance, approximate brightness, wake-lock use, active screen, signal conditions, start/end battery percentage, perceived heat, and route/free-roam mode. Any opt-in developer diagnostics added for this must not retain location/speed history and must not become analytics/telemetry, per this project's explicit non-goals.
      2. **Low-risk rendering audit:** inspect for unnecessary React rerenders, MapLibre repaint/camera-easing frequency, hidden/off-screen chart or map work, and network tile churn. Require a measured, reproducible issue before changing anything found here.
      3. **Possible battery-saving presentation, gated on evidence from stages 1–2:** consider a manually selectable dark/OLED-friendly active-Riding theme and a compatible dark/dim map style, preserving outdoor contrast, route/warning colours, attribution/licensing requirements, and accessibility. A dark UI alone does not address GPS/rendering cost or a bright map style, and must not be described as a proven fix by itself.
      4. **Higher-risk power changes, gated on comparative field evidence only:** do not lower GPS accuracy, weaken manoeuvre timing, degrade Follow reliability, or silently disable wake lock. Any such option must be explicit, reversible, off by default, and tested against navigation accuracy as well as battery use before being offered.
    - Keep the investigation (stages 1–2) and any eventual feature (stages 3–4) as clearly separate future slices, each with its own acceptance evidence — do not implement stage 3/4 work on the strength of the single two-hour ride reported here.

### Platform compatibility

61. **Android GPX share-sheet import feasibility**
    - Field observation: on Android, opening a GPX file received through WhatsApp currently offers only `Open with` choices (no ability to save to Files first), and the installed ACN PWA is not offered as an option there.
    - Platform distinction (re-verify against current official documentation before acting on it, since browser capability support changes over time): the web File Handling API (which can register a PWA as a file's default handler for an `Open with` chooser) is documented by Chrome as desktop-only today — it is not expected to make an installed Android PWA appear in an `Open with .gpx` chooser. The Web Share Target API is a distinct mechanism that can let an installed Android PWA appear as a target in a genuine Android Share/Send sheet — this depends on WhatsApp/Android actually exposing a Share/Send action for the received file, which is unconfirmed for this exact flow. A static GitHub Pages PWA can potentially implement a Web Share Target through its manifest plus service-worker/Workbox request handling, without an application server — this remains to be prototyped, not assumed to already work. This cannot make ACN appear in an `Open with`-only chooser under any of the above; it only helps if a genuine Share/Send path exists for this file. Useful, but not yet re-verified, official starting points: <https://developer.chrome.com/docs/capabilities/web-apis/file-handling>, <https://web.dev/web-share-target>, <https://web.dev/articles/workbox-share-targets> — check current status before relying on any detail from them.
    - Confirmed repository state: no Web Share Target, File Handling API, `share_target`/`file_handlers` manifest field, or related service-worker route exists anywhere in the current codebase (confirmed by an exhaustive case-insensitive grep). This is a fresh feasibility item, not a partially-built feature.
    - Stage the work, gated at each step:
      1. **Real-device flow capture and feasibility:** obtain screenshots or a screen recording of the exact WhatsApp menu/chooser on the user's real Android device, plus Android/Chrome/WhatsApp version numbers, to determine whether a genuine Share/Send path exists at all. Record the result as real-device evidence, distinct from any later Chromium-emulated Playwright coverage.
      2. **Static prototype, only if step 1 finds a Share path:** prove an installed Android PWA can register as a file share target under the project's current Vite PWA/Workbox configuration and GitHub Pages base-path deployment, covering both app-closed and app-already-open invocation.
      3. **Local GPX import integration, only if step 2 succeeds:** accept `.gpx` and realistic MIME types via the share target, validate through the existing file-size/XML/coordinate/import pipeline unchanged, never upload the file anywhere, and show a clear foreground review/confirmation or error state. Any service-worker handoff buffering the shared file must be short-lived and must not retain personal GPX data indefinitely.
      4. **Conflict and recovery coverage:** an inbound share must respect the existing unfinished route/free-roam conflict guard (item 42's fail-closed `checkFreeRoamConflict`-style pattern) and must never silently replace an active session or Planning draft; it must work after a fresh install/relaunch. Add Playwright coverage where meaningful, then require real Android Chrome acceptance — Chromium/Playwright emulation cannot prove real OS share-sheet registration, matching this project's existing, repeatedly-stated distinction between the `android-chrome` Playwright project and genuine physical-device verification (item 25).
    - If step 1 finds only an `Open with` path and no Share/Send path, document that conclusively and record that no pure static-PWA solution is currently available for this exact flow — do not propose a native wrapper, Trusted Web Activity, APK, or other Android-specific packaging as a workaround; that would be a major architecture departure and is explicitly not approved by this backlog item.
    - Requires the "## Explicit non-goals" edit recorded above (item 61's own cross-reference).

### End-to-end test reliability

62. **Unbounded `apt`-based Playwright browser-dependency install could hang CI indefinitely — done**
    - Two consecutive `Deploy to GitHub Pages` runs for commit `515059c` (v0.3.44) stalled at the same step, [run 32232770692](https://github.com/adf-MD/amazing-cycling-navigation/actions/runs/32232770692) (`ubuntu-24.04` image `20260816.277.1`, runner `GitHub Actions 1000000258`) and a second attempt on a different runner (`GitHub Actions 1000000260`). Every repository-controlled check — checkout, pinned Node/npm toolchain verification, `npm ci`, formatting, linting, type checking, 2,604 unit/component tests, and the production build (~10s) — succeeded both times; the workflow then ran `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium`, which never progressed past downloading Chromium. `--with-deps` shells out to `apt-get` to install the browser's OS-level dependencies; `azure.archive.ubuntu.com` stopped responding, fallback metadata trickled in from `archive.ubuntu.com`, and package-index retrieval then made no further progress until the run was manually cancelled — a genuine Azure Ubuntu-mirror/runner-image failure, not an application regression (the previous successful deployment used image `20260810.271.1`; prior successful runs' own Playwright install steps had taken approximately 21–51 seconds). The repository's own build was never at fault; the defect was that the pipeline performed an unbounded, live `apt-get` operation on every single push with no `timeout-minutes` anywhere, so a mirror outage blocked deployment indefinitely with no clear failure signal, rather than merely failing fast and legibly.
    - **Chosen fix — architectural, not a retry.** `apt-get`/`--with-deps` is removed from the pipeline entirely rather than retried or worked around. `.github/workflows/deploy-pages.yml`'s single `build` job is split into two independent jobs feeding `deploy`: **`verify`** (`ubuntu-latest`, unchanged — checkout, pinned-toolchain check, `npm ci`, format/lint/typecheck/unit tests/build, then `configure-pages`/`upload-pages-artifact`; Playwright is no longer installed here at all) and **`e2e`** (`ubuntu-latest`, running inside `container: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.1-noble` — the official Playwright Docker image, versioned 1:1 with this repository's pinned `"playwright-core": "1.61.1"`, with the matching Chromium build and every OS dependency already baked in). `e2e` runs its own `actions/setup-node` (same `.nvmrc`-pinned version, verified independently via the same pinned-toolchain check `verify` already used — both jobs run repository commands, so both verify the toolchain deliberately, per this file's own change-discipline), `npm ci`, a new guard step comparing the installed `playwright-core` version against the image tag (`node -p "require('./node_modules/playwright-core/package.json').version"`, mirroring the existing Node/npm toolchain check's own fail-fast `::error::` style — catches a future `@playwright/test` bump that forgets to also bump this workflow's image tag, before it can fail confusingly deep inside `npm run e2e`), its own independent `npm run build` (to drive its own `vite preview` server; only `verify`'s build is what's actually uploaded/deployed), then `npm run e2e`. **No `playwright install` step exists anywhere in this job.** `deploy` now depends on `needs: [verify, e2e]` (was `needs: build`) — skipped entirely if either fails, times out or is cancelled, so a Pages artifact can never publish without a passing build and a passing E2E run from the same triggering commit. `verify` and `e2e` run in parallel (no `needs` between them, each performing its own checkout/build) rather than sequentially, since this is a low-frequency, single-maintainer repository where CI-minute cost is not a binding constraint and `deploy`'s own gate is unaffected by ordering.
    - **Why not other approaches.** Avoiding `--with-deps` while staying on the plain GitHub-hosted image (browser download only, no OS deps) was rejected — it still assumes the runner image's OS libraries stay Chromium-compatible indefinitely with no verification, which is exactly what changed between image `20260810.271.1` and `20260816.277.1`. Retrying/rewriting Ubuntu mirrors was rejected — still depends on a live `apt-get` at CI time and is the install-step analogue of the "retries/`continue-on-error`/deployment bypass" anti-patterns this file already avoids elsewhere. Pinning a different runner label or older image was rejected — no evidence a different `ubuntu-*` label avoids this specific mirror/image interaction, and a temporary downgrade has no clean removal condition, unlike the structurally superior, already-proven container fix.
    - **Permissions and timeouts.** The workflow-level `permissions:` block (`contents: read`, `pages: write`, `id-token: write`) is left exactly as it already was, applying to all three jobs — this is the existing, working configuration; narrowing `pages`/`id-token` to just `deploy` is a legitimate idea but untested here and not required to fix this incident, so it was deliberately not bundled in. `verify` gained `timeout-minutes: 15` and `e2e` gained `timeout-minutes: 20`, informed by this incident's own timing evidence (build ~10s; the now-eliminated install step's prior 21–51s) plus locally measured `npm ci`/build/e2e timings — wide enough to absorb normal variance on a GitHub-hosted runner (which has far fewer cores than any local proof environment) while still firmly exposing a genuine multi-hour infrastructure hang rather than masking it. `deploy` itself was deliberately left without its own `timeout-minutes`: a prior attempt to change `actions/deploy-pages@v5`'s own internal timeout (commit `084ff3d`) was reverted (commit `85f92ef`) after being shown not to address a separate, already-investigated `deploy-pages` timing issue — out of this item's own scope. No `--ipc=host`/`--shm-size` container option was added speculatively (no evidence of a `/dev/shm` failure mode against this exact image); no Playwright-browser caching was added (none is needed, since the browsers are baked into the image, not downloaded); the existing `cache: npm` behaviour in both jobs is unchanged and keyed deterministically off `package-lock.json`'s hash, unrelated to Playwright package/browser versions.
    - **Local verification before pushing**, using Docker in the environment that authored this fix (network egress to `mcr.microsoft.com`/`nodejs.org`/GitHub release assets confirmed available there): pulled `mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.1-noble` and confirmed its `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH=/ms-playwright` already contains a `chromium-1228` revision folder identical to the one this repository's own pinned `@playwright/test@1.61.1` installs locally, confirming the image tag and the npm-pinned version resolve to the same browser build; confirmed neither `playwright-core` nor `playwright`'s `package.json` declares a `postinstall` script, so a plain `npm ci` never triggers a browser download or `apt` call on its own; installed the exact pinned Node v24.18.0 tarball inside the container and confirmed it bundles npm 11.16.0 exactly, matching this repository's pin with no drift; copied the real working tree into the container (never touching the host checkout) and, using only the pinned Node/npm, ran `npm ci` (~68s, zero `apt`/browser-download activity in the log), `npx playwright --version` → `1.61.1` (instant, no network), `npm run build` (~12s, matching this incident's own evidence), then the complete `npm run e2e` suite — **161/161 tests passed** across both the `chromium` (147) and `android-chrome` (14) Playwright projects, with zero installation steps of any kind. The drafted workflow YAML itself was validated with `actionlint v1.7.7` (0 errors) and `PyYAML` (parses cleanly; confirmed `deploy.needs == [verify, e2e]`). Formatting, linting, type checking, the full unit/component suite and the production build were also run through this repository's own required local gate before committing (see this file's own standing note that the sandbox's local Node/npm do not exactly match the pinned toolchain — an existing, unrelated, already-accepted limitation, not introduced by this slice).
    - **Real GitHub Actions confirmation**, commit `c6901ee`, [run 32241084913](https://github.com/adf-MD/amazing-cycling-navigation/actions/runs/32241084913): `Verify and build` (plain `ubuntu-latest`, no container) succeeded in 2m51s (10:07:06–10:09:57 UTC) — format, lint, typecheck, 2,604 unit/component tests, build, `configure-pages`, `upload-pages-artifact`. `End-to-end tests` (the new `mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.1-noble` container job) had its checkout/`setup-node`/pinned-toolchain-check/`npm ci`/Playwright-version-guard/build steps all succeed cleanly on both attempts below, with zero `apt`-based install activity in either — the incident this item fixes did not recur. First attempt: 160/161 tests passed in 5.6m, with one isolated failure (`e2e/ridingCamera.spec.ts:136`, a pre-existing camera-settle timing assertion — see item 63 below); `deploy` correctly skipped, proving the "never deploy without a passing E2E run" invariant holds. A "Re-run failed jobs" attempt (no code change, same commit) reused `verify`'s already-successful result and re-ran `e2e` alone: 161/161 passed in 6m41s (10:23:19–10:30:00 UTC); `deploy` then ran and succeeded in 11s (10:30:04–10:30:15 UTC), confirmed via GitHub's own deployments API as deployment `5980601849` for commit `c6901ee`, and the live site (`https://adf-md.github.io/amazing-cycling-navigation/`) returns HTTP 200.
    - This is CI/documentation hardening only: no production code, application test, dependency version (Node/npm/Playwright stay exactly pinned), or application version changed. The separate, still-open `RouteLibrary.test.tsx` focus-timing flake (see this file's own repeated mentions of it, most recently alongside item 46's verification) is untouched and remains open.

63. **`e2e/ridingCamera.spec.ts`'s "re-pressing Follow location..." test: a genuine, confirmed race — done**
    - **Superseded classification.** This item previously described a single occurrence (commit `c6901ee`, [run 32241084913](https://github.com/adf-MD/amazing-cycling-navigation/actions/runs/32241084913)) as "isolated, unreproduced," reasoning that a passing immediate rerun proved the failure wasn't a real defect. That reasoning was wrong and is corrected here: a **second, independent occurrence** landed on commit `f69c8d6` ("Fix RouteLibrary pin/rename focus-restoration stranding races"), [run 32249761600](https://github.com/adf-MD/amazing-cycling-navigation/actions/runs/32249761600) — the exact same final assertion (`e2e/ridingCamera.spec.ts:172`, `expect.poll(...).toBe(true)` timing out after 5000ms, the four camera properties never re-converging on the values captured when Follow first settled), on a diff that touches only Route Library focus-restoration code, `CLAUDE.md`, and the patch version — nowhere near Riding, MapLibre, or camera code. A rerun passing proves non-determinism, not the absence of a defect; a second independent failure on an unrelated commit removes any doubt that a real, if narrow, race exists.
    - **Confirmed root cause**, via direct instrumentation (temporary `console.log` calls added to `mapAdapter.ts`'s `resize()`/`setCamera()`/`onCameraSettled()`, logging the container's `getBoundingClientRect()` and every camera value at each call, then run repeatedly and removed before the final commit): `.ride-map-container--immersive`'s flex-fill box genuinely resizes during the first second or so of an active ride — observed growing from 318px to 396px tall shortly after `Start riding`, well before either Follow ease fires, as the shared status stack above it (header, wake-lock control, status strip, next-manoeuvre panel) settles into its final layout. `MapLibreGlMap.resize()` (confirmed by direct inspection of the installed `maplibre-gl@6` source) recomputes the transform's cached container width/height and additionally fires its own synthetic `movestart`/`move`/`moveend` sequence whenever the camera isn't already animating; `MapView.tsx`'s `ResizeObserver` calls `mapRef.current?.resize()` asynchronously on every container size change. Critically, `setCamera`'s `easeTo({..., offset: [0, 60], ...})` for a live "following" command (`followOffset: true`) converts that pixel offset into a geographic delta using MapLibre's transform dimensions *as cached at the exact synchronous moment `easeTo()` is called* — and `MapView.tsx`'s `cameraTarget` effect (the one driving Riding's Follow/North-up commands) had a confirmed architectural asymmetry versus its sibling `boundsTarget` effect in the same file: `boundsTarget` explicitly calls `mapRef.current?.resize()` immediately before `fitBounds(...)` (with an existing comment already describing exactly this staleness risk), while `cameraTarget` went straight to `setCamera(...)` with no such resync. If the container's cached dimensions differ between the moment the *first* Follow ease fires (right as `style.load` settles, potentially before the layout above has fully stabilised) and the moment the *second*, explicit re-Follow ease fires (well after the manual-gesture sequence, once everything has visually settled), the two `easeTo` calls compute geographically different offset-adjusted centres from an otherwise byte-identical command — diverging by more than the test's deliberately tight `1e-6`-degree tolerance. Direct instrumentation confirmed the mechanism quantitatively: across 20 sequential local runs, both `setCamera` calls always used an identical, already-settled container rect (the safety margin between the 428ms layout-settle and the ~750ms geolocation-delivery latency was comfortable in this environment), and the two follows' settled centres differed only by the same ~1e-13-degree floating-point noise the test's own header comment already documents as expected — directly demonstrating that when the two calls share the same container rect, results converge to noise level, and by construction diverge whenever they don't. This cleanly explains why only the **followOffset** (Follow-location) test ever showed this failure and never the offset-`[0,0]` North-up test in the same file (items 21/44): a zero pixel offset has nothing for stale dimensions to mis-convert. `RidingWakeLockControl` — the leading hypothesis at the start of this investigation, since item 56 already found and fixed an identical symptom caused by the "Map follow paused" toast resizing this same flex-fill box — was directly ruled out: `useScreenWakeLock`'s `desired` defaults to `false` for a fresh ride and this test never touches the checkbox, so its `status === "active"` span (the only dynamic content in that control) never mounts.
    - **Fix**, `src/map/MapView.tsx`: the `cameraTarget` effect now calls `mapRef.current?.resize();` immediately before `mapRef.current?.setCamera(...)`, mirroring `boundsTarget`'s existing, already-proven ordering exactly — applied unconditionally (not gated on `followOffset`) for consistency with that precedent and to cover any future offset-sensitive command sharing this effect. `mapAdapter.ts`'s `resize()` is cheap and idempotent, so this closes the staleness window on every explicit Follow/North-up application regardless of what caused the container to resize, without touching the reducer (`rideCamera.ts`), the hook (`useRideCamera.ts`), `mapAdapter.ts` itself, or the other three explicit-command effects (`centreTarget`/`orientNorthTarget`/`zoomTarget`), none of which had any evidence implicating them.
    - **Test hardening**, `e2e/ridingCamera.spec.ts`: the "re-pressing Follow location..." test's baseline capture (`followedCentre`/`followedBearing`/`followedPitch`/`followedZoom`, previously four separate sequentially-awaited `getAttribute()` Playwright round-trips) and its final `expect.poll(...)` body now both read all four `data-camera-*` attributes via one atomic `page.evaluate()` call (`readCameraAttributesAtomically`), so a `moveend` landing between what were four independent async round-trips can never tear the snapshot across two different settle events — an independent test-quality gap, closed regardless of the production root cause above.
    - **Deterministic regression coverage**, `src/map/MapView.test.tsx`: two new tests — `"resizes the map before applying a cameraTarget's setCamera"` (mirrors `"resizes the map before fitting a boundsTarget's bounds"` exactly, including its `points={[]}` isolation from the route-overview fit's own resize-then-fitBounds pair) and `"resizes the map again before re-applying a cameraTarget with a new requestId"` (the actual re-Follow scenario: byte-identical values, only a fresh `requestId`, proving `resize()` fires again on the second application too, not just the first). Both confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code with the exact predicted symptom (`resizeSpy` never called) via a temporary `git stash` of only the production change, then restored and re-verified green.
    - **Verification.** Reproduction of the pre-fix race itself remained elusive locally despite bounded, systematic attempts — informative in its own right, not a gap papered over: 75/75 passed under ordinary 4-worker parallel load; 15/15 under `taskset -c 0-3` CPU-constrained contention (mirroring items 25/32's own established method); 20/20 inside the exact pinned `mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.1-noble` CI container image; and under an intentionally extreme `--cpus=2`/10-worker oversubscription inside that same container, all repeats failed but at an earlier, unrelated assertion (the very first Follow settle timing out), not the specific narrow re-Follow divergence — blunt CPU starvation reproduces general flakiness, not this specific race, which plausibly depends on a narrow relative-timing window (async `ResizeObserver`/layout/geolocation-delivery timing) that local contention doesn't reliably recreate. This fix is therefore applied on the strength of the direct instrumentation evidence above plus the two real CI failures — matching this file's own established precedent (e.g. item 46's closed-loop fix) for shipping a fix on CI evidence plus rigorous, quantitative static/instrumented analysis when live reproduction proves elusive. Post-fix: the exact previously-failing test passed 45/45 under `--repeat-each=15 --workers=1`, 45/45 under `--repeat-each=15 --workers=6`, and 45/45 inside the pinned Docker container under `--repeat-each=15 --workers=6`; neighbouring camera/restore coverage (`e2e/freeRoam.spec.ts`, `e2e/ridingImmersiveShell.spec.ts`, `e2e/ridingMapProfileViews.spec.ts`, 28 tests) all passed; focused unit/component suites (`rideCamera.test.ts`, `useRideCamera.test.ts`, `mapAdapter.test.ts`, `MapView.test.tsx`, `RidingScreen.test.tsx`, `FreeRoamScreen.test.tsx`, `useFreeRoamCamera.test.ts`) all passed. Full gate: `npm run format`, `npm run lint`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm run build`, `npm test` (2,608/2,608 across 136 files — one unrelated `src/gpx/parseAcnExtension.test.ts` timeout was observed once under this shared, externally-loaded machine's full-suite parallel run, confirmed pre-existing per this file's own item 47/49 precedent for this exact test — 50/50 pass in isolation, and a full clean re-run of the entire suite passed 2,608/2,608), `npm run e2e` (161/161 across both the `chromium` and `android-chrome` projects), `npm run format:check` run last, immediately before committing — all pass.
    - Since this changes production camera behaviour (`MapView.tsx`), version bumped to 0.3.46 (`package.json` and `package-lock.json`'s two matching `version` fields, hand-patched, following the established precedent).
    - No real-device iPhone or Android verification is claimed for this slice — this is automated-browser reliability work.

### Component test reliability

64. **`RouteLibrary.test.tsx`'s recurring focus-timing flake (items 25/46/62) — done**
    - **Recurrence history, corrected.** First observed item 25 (v0.3.16): "a single unrelated `RouteLibrary.test.tsx` focus-timing flake was observed under full-suite parallel load and confirmed pre-existing (44/44 pass in isolation)." Recurred item 46 (v0.3.34): "one `RouteLibrary.test.tsx` focus-timing test failed once under full-suite parallel load... 46/46 pass in isolation... matching the identical known flake item 33 already documented" — that cross-reference is itself inaccurate (item 33's own text, "Compact Route Library grouping," reports a clean run and never mentions a flake); left uncorrected there, per this file's own preference for the smallest evidence-backed change. Reaffirmed still-open by item 62 ("The separate, still-open `RouteLibrary.test.tsx` focus-timing flake... is untouched and remains open"). A further live recurrence was reported during this slice's own handover, described as happening "during item 56 verification" — checked directly against item 56's own text (the Riding Map/Profile immersive-shell item) and found not to be there at all; item 56's own verification paragraph reports a clean `2604/2604` with no RouteLibrary mention. The true documented chain is 25 → 46 → 62; the "56" attribution is corrected here rather than perpetuated.
    - **Root cause 1 — pin-focus stranding, a real production defect.** `RouteLibrary.tsx`'s pin-focus-restoration effect (added by item 33) depended on `[groups, pinPendingIds]`, but the pin toggle's `disabled` (`RouteListItem.tsx`) is `isPinPending || isDeleting` — a third, untracked input, since `isDeleting` is one shared boolean covering every row, not just the one being deleted. If an unrelated route's `isDeleting → false` transition landed with no accompanying `groups`/`pinPendingIds` change, the effect never re-ran and a pending pin-focus marker could strand permanently. Fixed by adding `isDeleting` as a third dependency, read the same indirect way as `pinPendingIds` (only via `button.disabled`) — the direct extension of item 33's own fix to the one remaining disabling input it didn't cover.
    - **Root cause 2 — rename-focus premature consumption, a second real production defect.** The rename-focus marker (`lastRenamedIdRef`) was unconditionally nulled on _any_ render where `viewRoutes`/`routes` changed while it was set — including a render caused by something unrelated (another route's pin/unpin, a search-query change) landing before the rename's own write had reached the live query. When the genuine rename update arrived later, the marker was already gone and the resulting orphaned focus was never corrected. Fixed by adding `lastRenamedNameRef` (the name `handleRename` actually wrote) and only consuming the marker once `routes` genuinely reflects the rename's own outcome — the route's name matches what was written, or the route no longer exists — never on an unrelated `viewRoutes`/`routes` change in between. `handleRename`'s `.catch()` now also clears both refs on a failed write (only if they're still the ones that call itself set, preserving the existing "second rename overwrites the marker" behaviour), closing a new latent edge case this fix would otherwise introduce: a failed write's outcome can never become "known" to the consuming effect.
    - **Confirmed not defects, by direct reading, not left as an omission.** Deletion focus (`handleDeleteConfirm`) computes its target synchronously from the pre-delete `viewRoutes` snapshot and always resolves to an already-mounted, stable fallback chain (a surviving route's button, or the always-rendered heading/search input) — no scenario leaves it targeting something temporarily disabled or not-yet-existing with no retry path. Rename-mode enter/exit, delete-cancel and search-clear are all purely local `useState`/synchronous handlers with no live-query dependency. Neither received a production change.
    - **Test hardening, independent of the two production fixes.** 7 of the file's 12 `toHaveFocus()` assertions asserted synchronously immediately after a `waitFor` on an _unrelated_ postcondition (DOM-node removal, or filtered-empty text) rather than the focus state itself — a pure test-side race against the live-query round trip. Each now wraps its own `toHaveFocus()` in its own `waitFor`, mirroring this file's own three pre-existing pin/unpin tests that already used this pattern and have never been reported flaking. The two delete-confirmation cancel/Escape tests were confirmed already safe (`handleDeleteCancel` is synchronous local state, no storage write, no live-query dependency anywhere in either path) and left unchanged.
    - **New deterministic regression coverage**, both confirmed to fail against the pre-fix production code with the exact predicted symptom (a `toHaveFocus()` timeout) and to pass once restored: "a pin write that resolves while an unrelated delete is in flight is not stranded once the delete settles" (both `pinRoute` and `deleteRoute` mocked with independently-resolvable deferred promises, so the exact interleaving — pin click while the toggle is still enabled, delete confirmed next, the pin write landing only once `isDeleting` is already true, then the delete settling — is deterministic rather than dependent on real IndexedDB timing); "an intervening unrelated re-render does not drop the pending rename-focus marker before the write lands" (a deferred `renameRoute` mock held pending across an unrelated route's real pin/unpin, proving the marker survives until the rename's own write actually lands).
    - **Reproduction, genuinely clean.** An initial 10-run bounded attempt was contaminated by this slice's own concurrent file-editing and test invocations (production-code and test-file edits landed mid-loop, and running isolated verification runs alongside it added CPU contention) — discarded rather than reported, since it would have been misleading either way. Two clean 10-run loops were then run back-to-back with the working tree held static and no other process running concurrently: **pre-fix**, 1 of 10 runs directly reproduced the real historical flake — `"renaming the only route matching the active filter moves focus to the search field"` failed with `Expected element with focus: <input id="route-library-search">`, `Received element with focus: <body>` — an exact match to the predicted test-awaiting-defect failure mode, on one of the same 7 tests fixed above; 5 of 10 runs additionally hit an unrelated, already-documented `src/gpx/parseAcnExtension.test.ts` timeout (see item 47's own verification note) at a notably higher rate than that note recorded, consistent with this sandbox carrying more background CPU pressure than the environment that first observed it — not investigated further here, out of this item's own scope. **Post-fix**, the identical clean-loop method produced zero `RouteLibrary.test.tsx` failures across all 10 runs; 3 of 10 hit the same unrelated `parseAcnExtension.test.ts` timeout, confirming it is independent of this fix.
    - Files changed: `src/ui/library/RouteLibrary.tsx`, `src/ui/library/RouteLibrary.test.tsx`.
    - **Verification.** `npx vitest run src/ui/library/RouteLibrary.test.tsx` — 48/48 (46 existing, 2 new). `npm run format`, `npm run lint`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm run build`, `npm test` — 2606/2606 across 136 files, `npm run e2e` — 161/161 across both the `chromium` and `android-chrome` projects, `npm run format:check` run last, immediately before committing — all pass. Version bumped to 0.3.45 (`package.json` and `package-lock.json`'s two matching `version` fields, hand-patched, following the established precedent). This is a pure logic/test-integrity fix with no user-visible behaviour change; real-device verification is not applicable.
